HQ P & SS (UK)
UNIDETIFIED FLYING OBJECES IN VALES
We have had two recent letters from Mr Nicholas Edwards MP, the Member for
Pembroke, about local enquiries he has received on the subject of Unidentificd
Flying Objects.
We have also had a number of letters direct from members of the
public in Wales (although not fron residents of Pembroke) and in addition there
have been one or two quite lurid press reports. I attach copies of the relevant
correspondence.
2. We have not invoked the assistance of P & SS before on UFOs and as I
explained on the telephone the last thing I want to do is to involve you in
extraneous problems which wouid divert you iron vour more
immediate work on low
various specialist authorities in MOD to make sure they contain no items of defence
interest but to leave it there. We have folloved the usual procedure in this case
and nothing has come to light to suggest anything untoward in Wales and in the
normal way we would have left it at that. I confess, however, that I find the
. correspondence little puzzling.
Mr Edwards does say in his letter of 24th May that he has been inundated with
enquiries (I should add however that the
Section 40e quotes is know to be associated
I should be grateful if you would have a look at these papers and let me mov
whether you think some sort of discreet enquiry would be worthwhile and if so
whether your staff could find time for it in view of your othor pressing obligations.
What I would really like to know is the volume of local interest and/or alarm and
whether there is a readily discernible rational explanation for it (perhaps a
practical joker) or even whether there is prima facie evidence for a more serious
specialist enquiry. But I have not committed you in anyway and I have not even
told the Minister I am consulting you
YOU think a P & SS initiative is likel
to be unfruitful I will drop the
bald you please let me know what you thin
if for de dice to go aleari.
caker

HQ P & SS (UK)
UNTDENTIFED FLYING OBJECTS IN MALES
We have had two recent letters from Mr Nicholas Edwards MP, the Member for
Pembroke, about local enquiries he has received on the subject of Unidentified
lying Objects. We have also had a number of letters direct fron nembers of the
public in Wales (although not fron residents of Pembroke) and in addition there
have been one or two quite lurid press reports. I attach copies of the relevant
correspondence.
2. We have not invoked the assistance of P & SS before on UFOs and as I
explained on the telephone the last thing I want to do is to involve you in
extraneous problems which would divert you from your more imnediate vork on lov
flying complaints. The standard procedure on UFOs is to send the reports to the
various specialist authorities in MOD to make sure they contain no items of defence
interest but to leave it there. We have folloved the usual procedure in this case
and nothing has come to light to suggest anything untovard in wales and in ths
normal way we would have left it at that
I confess, however, that I find the
correspondence a little puzzling.
According to the press cuttings some of the
"witnerses" are people who night be expected to be fairly level headed and
Mr Edwards does say in his letter of 24th May that he has been inundated with
enquiries (I should add however that the
he quotes is know to be associated
with a local UFO society and so ray have a vested interest).
I should be grateful if you would have a look at these papers and let me know
whether you think some sort of discreet enquiry would be worthwhile and if so
whether your staff could find time for it in view of your other pressing obligations.
What I would really like to know is the volume of local interest and/or alarm and
whether there is a readily discernible rational explanation for it (perhars a
practical joker) or even whether there is prina facie evidence for a nore serious.
specialist enquiry. But I have not comnitted you in anyway and I have not even
told the Minister I am consulting you. If you think a P & SS initiative is likely
to be unfruitful I will drop the idea. Would you please let me know what you think.
14 Jun 77

===== PAGE 36 =====
{"alpha_words": 209, "chars": 1263, "method": "ocr", "page": 36, "variant": "plain-vision"}
DRAFT
— Letter from US of S(RAF) to Nicholas Edwards MP
Thank you for your letter of 24th May about the enquiries you have received
recently on the phenomena which are thought to be Unidentified Flying Objects.
The Ministry of Defence has not the resources to conduct an objective scientific
enquiry into the nature of UFOs and we do not undertake to identify objects that
may be reported by members of the public. For obvious reasons, however, we look.
into detailed reports of unexplained phenomena to see if they have a bearing on
the national defences. The Department have carried out that type of investigation
on the reports received from Wales but these have been few in recent months and
none has come from Pembrokeshire. No evidence has been revealed to suggest that
the alleged sightings in Wales, or indeed those reported from other parts of
• Great Britain in the past, contained anything of significance from a defence point
of view. All I can tell you on the basis'of the Department's past experience is
that most reports oi this nature can usually be referred to a common place object
which may have been observed perhaps from an unusual angle or in somewhat
unusual weather or lighting conditions. I hope this helps to reassure your
constituents.

RE: Dr Clarke's request
Fyl do not hold any info on meteors, comets, etc. We have accurate info for all large object (>1 sq m) re-entries but
Data on small object re-entries are not so accurate: We are provided with a decay date (but no time) for these
events, and the accuracy of these dates cannot be given high credence (most are probably OK +/- 1 day).
Between 23 - 25 March 97 we have details of two small object re-entries:
Object 24010 (94029 BF) and 24497 (94029 UW).
These were fragments from a pegasus rocket 700-piece break-up. Both fragments will have been very small. Most
from this break-up were thin shards of carbon-fibre wire a tew centimetres in length.

Thank you for ycur letter of 24th May about the enquiries you have received
recently on the phenomena which are thought to be Unidentified Flying Objects.
The Ministry of Defence has not the resources to conduct an objective scientific
enquiry into the nature of UFOs and we do not undertake to identify objects that
may be reported by members of the public. For obvious reasons, however, we look
into detailed reports of unexplained phenomena to see if they have a bearing on
the national defences. The Department have carried out that type of investigation
on the reports received from Wales but these have been few in recent months and
none has come from Pembrokeshire. No evidence has been revealed to suggest that
the alleged sightings in Wales, or indeed those reported from other parts of
• Great Britain in the past, contained anything of significance from a defence point
of view. All I can tell you on the basis of the Department's past experience is
that most reports of this nature can usually be referred to a common place object
which may have been observed perhaps from an unusual angle or in somewhat
unusual weather or lighting conditions. I hope this helps to reassure your
constituents.

REDACTION ON ORIGINAL DOCUMENT
1. How to react to further proposals by Grenada, following up their
1977 initiative on UFOs.
Background
2. At last year's General Assembly Premier Gairy of Grenada urged the
United Nations to establish an agency or working group for research
into unidentified flying objects. Mr
expressed the view that
the proposal would bring the UN into disrepute. Accordingly, the UK
delegation was instructed to oppose the draft Grenadan resolution. In
the event, no vote was taken on the resolution; as a compromise, the
General Assembly "studied the text" and requested the Secretary-Genera.
to transmit it to state members of the UN and to interested specialised
agencies so that they might communicate their views to the Secretary-
General.
3. The Secretary-General's subsequent report contained only three replie
from Governments, (India, Luxembourg and the Seychelles) and unforth-
C 4. The Permanent Mission of Grenada circulated a letter to Permanent
Missions in New York on 10 November, which was followed up by a statemeni
to the Special Political Committee on 27 November by the Prime Minister
of Grenada, who complicated matters by introducing a new resolution inste
of the one previously circulated.
in UKMIS telno 2566 of 27 November.

Vincluding s

(including the FRG Presidency and the United States) are making an
effort to help Grenada off the hook if this can be done without cost
to the UN. It must also be said, in fairness, that the Grenadan
proposal is no more ridiculous than many other proposals before the
UN. Indeed President Carter has in the past reportedly taken a
personal interest in the subject of UFOs. The proposal is at least
not positively harmful to UK interests, whereas taking too strong and
open a stand against Mr Gairy could be. Moreover, if we can help the
Grenadans to reach a compromise, it could conceivably help us to
persuade them to abstain on the vote on foreign economic and other
interests in plenary (the Fourth Committee Resolution which inter alia
strongly condems the UK and others for dealings with South Africa).
Recommendation

original Grenadan proposal but to give discreet support to efforts by
western or other countries to postpone or reduce the scope and cost
of the Grenadan proposal as much as possible. I accordingly submit a

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS
As you know, S4(Air) are responsible for answering public enquiries and for briefing
Ministers on MPs' questions about UFOs. I attach our stock reply which has been
repy li CDS A Corespirocié.
So far as I know there has been no British Scientific enquiry into the possible
existence of UFOs, nor is there evidence that MOD has taken the question very
seriously. However, we have kept in touch with the United States Air Force who
made a systematic, if protracted, attempt to record and investigate UFO sightings
by a study which was originally suggested by a member of a congressional investigation
in the Armed Forces Committee and which was assigned by the Air Force Office of
Scientific Research to the University of Colorado. It was led by Dr Edward V Condon,
Professor of Physics and formerly Director of the National Bureau of Standards.
This is the enquiry referred to in the attached brief and, as stated there, it
was endorsed in 1969 by the National Academy of Sciences who concluded that there
caused a good deal of controversy and, I believe, was much criticised. However,
when I consulted the RAF Staff in Washington about this they assured me in
August 1976 that HQ USAF saw no likelihood of their further involvement in this
area. The USAF pointed to the considerable Air Force commitment of resources in
the past, the extreme pressure on Air Force funds in 1976 and the fact that since
It is always possible of course, that the considerable correspondence addressed
terrestrial origin which could have a bearing on national security. We have
therefore been careful to refer all UFO enquiries from the public to ADI55 and DD
Ops (GE) (RAF) for any further investigation which they might consider to be justified.
As a Secretariat branch, S4(Air) does not need to know about this type of enquiry
and we do not ask for further information; we tell the public flatly that we
to the point of positive identification. The onty exception to this is the
and we seek ADI 55's advice so that the Minister can reply more fully and
publish the correspondence fros the public. The only reason for that is lack
of staff; we would have to obliterate the identities of our nany correspondenth,
and possibly occasional references to classified subjects, and there is no oue
we can spare for the job.

As it happens, however, I was about to seek confirmation of the general line

unaccountably. There was a sustained flurry in the 1960s which was sufficient to
embroil my predecessor in a BBC TV programme. Last summer there was some concern
in Wales although the RAF Police thought this could have been the work of a practical
joker. In the last few weeks the Prime Minister of Grenada has secured a UFO
debate at the United Nations (which could conceably involve the Foreign Office
and No 10 in correspondence) and he has also had discussions with Mr Jimmy Carter
whom the Press have connected with UFOs in the past. Then a few days ago the
Earl of Glancarty put down a number of questions in the House of Lords referring
to UFO investigations allegedly under way in France and a broadcast said to have
been made in 1974 by the Contemporary French Minister of Defence. (See Hansard
extracts attached).
We believe that
is a Vice President of the British UFO Research Association. I should
add that we were unable to verify the alleged Ministerial broadcast when a member
of the public told us in 1976 that in it the French had admitted the existence
of flying saucers. The British Air Attache in Paris did find out however that the
French operate a procedure under which UFO reports from the public go in the first
instance to the French Defence Ministry (Air) who study the military implications
and finally to a Government organisation known as the Centre Nationale D'Etude
Spatial where the reports are considered by scientists. The reports are also
fed into a computer where they are "added to the statistics of the problem'.
We were advised that the French military authorities had found nothing of an
aggressive nature in the sightings although their scientists had been unable to
explain the phenomena.
written to the Air Attache to document it and up date it in the light of the Earl
of Glancarty's questions.

EASTERN DAILY PRESS: OCT II G6
new theory about those strange lights in the sky
Stumped?
By STEVE DOWNES
Not any
The truth was out there.
But even agent Mulder of the X-Files would
find it hard to believe.
The great East Anglian UFO mystery was
solved last night - and turned out to be just a
storm and a church tower.
The weekend sightings of colourful flashing
lights over the Wash captured the imagination
of would-be X-Files sleuths across the region.
Letters and telephone calls flooded into the
EDP after the sightings, which coincided with
the appearance of a mysterious object on
radars.
One theory was that the culprit was planet
Venus.
But Norfolk RAF investigators have now
found logical explanations - and have ruled
out the possibility of little green men peering
down on us.
Flight Lt Keith Sweatman, of RAF Neatishead,
said: "We now know that the radar trace was
Boston Stump - the church tower at Boston.
"And the weather people said that the
coloured lights in the sky coincided with an
electrical storm over the Wash.
"You do get weird experiences with electrical
storms, and they can produce lots of different
colours."
The 200ft church tower would have been ruled
out if it had been picked up by a more
advanced RAF radar, which can computer-
enhance images.
But the air traffic control radar did not have
similar facilities, and operators jumped to the
conclusion that it must have been linked with
the tashing red, blue, green and white lights,
seen by the tanker Conocoast.
Following the weekend sightings, an
investigation was launched by the Ministry of
Defence to ensure they did not pose a threat to
national security. That has now been
discounted.
The mystery is expected to be cleared up once
and for all in the next couple of days when
experts at RAF Neatishead view video footage
of the lights. They are waiting for the video to
arrive after being sent from Boston police.
Flight Lt Sweatman stressed they would be
-keeping an open mind.
*I don't totally discount UFOs," he said.
iIm not a disbeliever, but I'm not a believer
either.
more... the
great Wash
UFO
Out of this world
sighting
finally
explained
FLASHBACK: How the EDP
reported the sighting on
Tuesday this week.

I have just received back the papers which we asked you to look at regarding possible release under the Code of
Practice on Access to Government Information (the Code).
I note that you are happy to release the text of most documents, but wish us to remove any references to people or
departments within the FCO. While we are happy to withhold individuals names (and any unique post identifiers) in
accordance with Exemption 12 of the Code (Privacy of an individual) and the Data Protection Act 1998, we do not
normally remove references to departments from documents we release. Indeed where appropriate we may even
release documents referring to Defence Intelligence branches. Under the Code it is not possible to mearly remove
details without sighting an Exemption of the Code, and being able to justify this action to the Parliamentary
Ombudsman should the requester challenge the decision, something I believe the person requesting this information
is likely to do, particularly if he is unable to tell where the documents have come from.
Please could you let me know as soon as possible, the reason for removing these details and what Exemption of the
Code you believe these should be witheld under.
If you prefer to talk to me, my telephone number is Section 40

ING OBJECTS
How to react to further proposals by Grenada, following up their
Background
2. At last year's General Assembly Premier Gairy of Grenada urged the
United Nations to establish an agency or working group for research
into unidentified flying objects. Mr Luard expressed the view that
the proposal would bring the UN into disrepute. Accordingly, the UK
the event, no vote was taken on the resolution; as a compromise, the
to transmit it to state members of the UN and to interested specialised
agencies so that they might communicate their views to the Secretary-
General.
3. The Secretary-General's subsequent report contained only three replie
from Governments, (India, Luxembourg and the Seychelles) and unforth-
C 4. The Permanent Mission of Grenada circulated a letter to Permanent
Missions in New York on 10 November, which was followed up by a statement
to the Special Political Committee on 27 November by the Prime Minister
D
of Grenada, who complicated matters by introducing a new resolution inste
of the one previously circulated. Subsequent developments are reported
Argument
6. The Grenadan initiative continues to hold no attractions whatsoever
Vincluding

===== PAGE 228 =====
{"alpha_words": 195, "chars": 1211, "method": "ocr", "page": 228, "variant": "plain-vision"}
(including the FRG Presidency and the United States) are making an
effort to help Grenada off the hook if this can be done without cost
to the UN. It must also be said, in fairness, that the Grenadan
proposal is no more ridiculous than many other proposals before the
UN. Indeed President Carter has in the past reportedly taken a
personal interest in the subject of UFOs. The proposal is at least
not positively harmful to UK interests, whereas taking too strong and
open a stand against Mr Gairy could be. Moreover, if we can help the
Grenadans to reach a compromise, it could conceivably help us to
persuade them to abstain on the vote on foreign economic and other
interests in plenary (the Fourth Committee Resolution which inter alia
strongly condems the UK and others for dealings with South Africa).
Recommendation
7. I recommend that we instruct the Mission to continue to resist the
original Grenadan proposal but to give discreet support to efforts by
Western or other countries to postpone or reduce the scope and cost
of the Grenadan proposal as much as possible. I accordingly submit a

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS AND RELATED PHENOMENA (continued) (A/33/141, A/33/268;
A/SPC/33/1.20)
Mr. FRIDAY (Grenada)* read out a letter from L. Gordon Cocper, a former
extraterrestrial vehicles whose crews vere visiting Tarth from more technically
advanced planets. Mr. Cooper had himself spent two days in 1951 observing UFOs of
different sizes flying in fighter formation, generally from east to west over
Europe. A top-level co-ordinated programme was needed to collect and analyse data
from all over the world concerning all types of encounters and to determine how best
to make friendly contact with such visitors. Man might have to prove to the
visitors that he had learned to solve his problems by peaceful means rather than by
warfare before he could be accepted as a fully qualified universal team member.
acceptance would have tremendous possibilities for the advancement of the world in
all areas, and it vould certainly seem that the United Nations had an interest in
handling the subject properly and expeditiously. If the United Nations agreed to
pursue the project, many more well-qualified people might agree to provide help and
information.
20
Mr. FRIEDMAN (Grenada)* said it was important to remember that men throughout
the vorld had something in common: they wero all earthlings, a fact which tended to
be forgotten when man's many differences were aired. After 20 years of study, he
believed that there was overwhelming evidence to suggest that the planet was being
visited by intelligently controlled entraterrestrial vehicles The evidence took the
form of eye-witness testimony from responsible persons, physical trace cases
producing changes in the environment observable long after the UFO had left, radar
visual sightings, photographs and abductions, apparently by aliens of earthlings
and the subsequent return of the earthlings, although there was no knowledge of
cases where earthlings had not been returned.
Every large-scale scientific study of UFOs had provided a substantial number of
cases in which the observations clearly indicated that the UFO was a manufactured
object behaving in ways which could not be duplicated by manufactured objects on
without any visible external engine, wings or tail, indicated that the spacecraft had
been manufactured by soneone other than man. Many people wished to know why UFOs
should visit Earth and what was happening on Earth that could be of interest to an
advanced civilization. Probably there were many things of interest to someone
concerned with the development of a primitive society. However, one thing was
cuaranteed to be of interest to another civilization and that vas the fact that
within one hundred years men vould be going to the stars. He himself, as a nuclear
physicist, had vorked on fission and fusion propulsion systems which would be
capable of transporting man to nearby star systems within reasonable periods of time,
and it would be obvious to any alien in the neighbourhood that man would be doing so
within a hundred years. Since the end of the Second World War, with the development
: iho full text of this statement will appear in document A/SPC/33/PV.35.

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS
You asked me to comment on loose minute DCDS (I)/52 dated 9 December 1977.
As you know, S4(Air) are responsible for answering public enquiries and for briefing
Ministers on MPs' questions about UFOs. I attach our stock reply which has been
refy li CDSs conesprocie.
So far as I know there has been no British Scientific enquiry into the possible
existence of UFOs, nor is there evidence that MOD has taken the question very
seriously. However, we have kept in touch with the United States Air Force who
made a systematic, if protracted, attempt to record and investigate UFO sightings
by a study which was originally suggested by a member of a congressional investigation
in the Armed Forces Committee and which was assigned by the Air Force Office of
Scientific Research to the University of Colorado. It was led by Dr Edward v Condon,
Professor of Physics and formerly Director of the National Bureau of Standards,
This is the enquiry referred to in the attached brief and, as stated there, it
was endorsed in 1969 by the National Academy of Sciences who concluded that there
reports of UFOs up to that time posed no threat to national security and that
continuance of the Blue Book Project was of doubtful value. The Condon report
caused a good deal of controversy and, I believe, was much criticised. However,
when I consulted the RAF Staff in Washington about this they assured me in
August 1976 that HQ USAF saw no likelihood of their further involvement in this
area. The USAF pointed to the considerable Air Force commitment of resources in
the past, the extreme pressure on Air Force funds in 1976 and the fact that since
It is always possible of course, that the considerable correspondence addressed
to MOD might contain some hint, not of UFOs, but of something with a hostile
terrestrial origin which could have a bearing on national security. We have
therefore been careful to refer all UFO enquiries from the public to ADI55 and DD
Ops (GE) (RAF) for any further investigation which they might consider to be justified.
As a Secretariat branch, S4(Air) does not need to know about this type of enquiry
and we do not ask for further information; we tell the public flatly that we
cannot enter into detailed correspondence, andienquiries are not necessarily made
The only exception to this is the
i i c cons
and we seek ADI 55's advice so that the Minister can reply more fully and
publish the correspondence fros the public. The only reason for that is lack
of staff; we would have to obliterate the identities of our nany correspondence,
and possibly occasional references to classified subjects, and there is no one
we can spare for the job.

a steady public interest in UFOs and from time to time it tends to increase
unaccountably. There was a sustained flurry in the 1960s which was sufficient to
Last summer there was some concern
in Wales although the RAF Police thought this could have been the work of a practical

to UFO investigations allegedly under way in France and a broadcast said to have
been made in 1974 by the Contemporary French Minister of Defence. (See Hansard
extracts attached).
6. We believe that
is a Vice President of the British UFO Research Association, I should
add that we were unable to verify the alleged Ministerial broadcast when a member
of the public told us in 1976 that in it the French had admitted the existence
of flying saucers. The British Air Attache in Paris did find out however that the
French operate a procedure under which UFO reports from the public go in the first
instance to the French Defence Ministry (Air) who study the military implications
and finally to a Government organisation known as the Centre Nationale D'Etude
Spatial where the reports are considered by scientists. The reports are also
fed into a computer where they are "added to the statistics of the problem'.
we were advised that the French military authorities had found nothing of an
aggressive nature in the sightings although their scientists had been unable to
explain the phenomena. As this information came to us by telephone I have now
written to the Air Attache to document it and up date it in the light of the Earl
of Glancarty's questions.
70
One way and another there could be renewed interest in UFOs in the next
few months and I have already been asked to take stock of our current policy.

272 Head of S4(Air) asks DI54 if the MoD

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS
Ministers on MPs' questions about UFOs. I attach our stock reply which has been
reply ls CBS A conspiracut.
So far as I know there has been no British Scientific enquiry into the possible
existence of UFOs, nor is there evidence that MOD has taken the question very
seriously. However, we have kept in touch with the United States Air Force who
made a systematic, if protracted, attempt to record and investigate UFO sightings
from 1952 to 1968. The USAF enquiry, known as Project Blue Book, was followed
by a study which was originally suggested by a member of a congressional investigation
in the Armed Forces Committee and which was assigned by the Air Force Office of
Scientific Research to the University of Colorado. It was led by Dr Edward V Condon,
Professor of Physics and formerly Director of the National Bureau of Standards.
This is the enquiry referred to in the attached brief and, as stated there, it
was endorsed in 1969 by the National Academy of Sciences who concluded that there
reports of UFOs up to that time posed no threat to national security and that
continuance of the Blue Book Project was of doubtful value. The Condon report
caused a good deal of controversy and, I believe, was much criticised. However,
August 1976 that HQ USAF saw no likelihood of their further involvement in this
The USAF pointed to the considerable Air Force commitment of resources in
the past, the extreme pressure on Air Force funds in 1976 and the fact that since
It is always possible of course, that the considerable correspondence addressed
to MOD might contain some hint, not of UFOs, but of something with a hostile
terrestrial origin which could have a bearing on national security. We have
therefore been careful to refer all UFO enquiries from the public to ADI55 and DD
Ops (GE) (RAF) for any further investigation which they might consider to be justified.
As a Secretariat branch, S4(Air) does not need to know about this type of enquiry
cannot enter into detailed correspondence, andiénquiries are not necessarily made
to the point of positive identification.
The only exception to this is the
occesional case where a Member of Parliament cheue prestional interest er concorn
specifically than the standard draft allows.
That is all there really #isto the MOD UFO "policy" qcept that we do not
publish the correspondence from the public. The only reason for that is lack
of staff; we would have to obliterate the identities of our nany correspondente,
and possibly occasional references to classified subjects, and there is no one
we can spare for the job.

===== PAGE 275 =====
{"alpha_words": 488, "chars": 2965, "method": "ocr", "page": 275, "variant": "plain-vision"}
As it happens, however, I was about to seek confirmation of the general line
we take even before you consulted me on the letter sent to CDS. There is always
a steady public interest in UFOs and from time to time it tends to increase
unaccountably. There was a sustained flurry in the 1960s which was sufficient to
in Wales although the RAF Police thought this could have been the work of a practical
joker. In the last few weeks the Prime Minister of Grenada has secured a UFO
debate at the United Nations (which could concerably involve the Foreign Office
and No 10 in correspondence) and he has also had discussions with Mr Jimmy Carter
whom the Press have connected with UFOs in the past. Then a few days ago the
Earl of Glancarty put down a number of questions in the House of Lords referring
to UFO investigations allegedly under way in France and a broadcast said to have
been made in 1974 by the Contemporary French Minister of Defence. (See Hansard
extracts attached).
6. We believe that the Earl's half brother and heir, the Hon Brinsley Le Poer
French, is a Vice President of the British UFO Research Association. I should
add that we were unable to verify the alleged Ministerial broadcast when a member
of the public told us in 1976 that in it the French had admitted the existence
of flying saucers. The British Air Attache in Paris did find out however that the
French operate a procedure under which UFO reports from the public go in the first
instance to the French Defence Ministry (Air) who study the military implications
and finally to a Government organisation known as the Centre Nationale D'Etude
Spatial where the reports are considered by scientists. The reports are also
fed into a computer where they are "added to the statistics of the problem'.
We were advised that the French military authorities had found nothing of an
aggressive nature in the sightings although their scientists had been unable to
explain the phenomena.
written to the Air Attache to document it and up date it in the light of the Earl
of Glancarty's questions.
One way and another there could be renewed interest in UFOs in the next
few months and I have already been asked to take stock of our current policy.
The basic questions are: -
b.
Is the general MOD policy valid (as outlined in the attached brief and
as supplemented above)?
Is it correct to confine MOD investigations to the defence implications
of alleged UFO sightings - should the investigations be extended either in
the interest of national defence or more widely into the more speculative
field concerning the possible existence of extra terrestrial, vehicles?
Should MOD take the public more seriously and, for example, visit the
places where sightings have been reported (a line of enquiry; I might add
I should be most grateful if you would seek the views of DSTI and Air
Cdre (Int) whom I believe you will be consulting before reporting to CDS.

D/S4(Aix)/8/2/1
UNIDENTIELED FLYING CRIECTS
1. Unidentified Flying Object (UFO) reports are received in the Ministry of Defence
from various sources, such-as members of the public, the police, and Service units.
The sole aim of the Ministry of Defence is to establish any possible defence
implications. We do not/undertake to puraue the investigation to the point of positive
correlation with a known object and there is no organisation appointed for the study
of UFOS, per se, UTO reports are examined by various вр staff within the
Ministry of Defence and MAP Commeede as part of their normal staff responsibilities.
Such staff have access to all information available to the Ministry of Defence. They
can call on the full scientific and professional resources of the Department and if
necessary may call on the expert advice of other Government and non-Government bodies.
Simple explanations have been found for the great najority of UFO reports, the most
common single source being aircraft or the lights of aircraft seen under unusual
conditions. Other common explanations are astrononical sightings, space satellites or
space junk, balloons, unusual cloud formations or aircraft condensation trails.
Unexplained reports have often béen found to be imprecise or lacking in some essential
data, but other wise to have been no different in kind from other reports.
2. Since the Ministry of Defence interest in UFOs is limited to the defence aspect a
study of the scientific significance of UFOs has rot been carried out, although ve are
of course aware of work that has been done elsewhere, in particular the studies of the
University of Colorado, whose main findings were endorsed by a panel of the US National
Academy of Sciences. The conclusions the panel cane to were:
DRAFT

===== PAGE 282 =====
{"alpha_words": 182, "chars": 1170, "method": "ocr", "page": 282, "variant": "plain-vision"}
a. about 90% of all UFO reports proved to be quite plausibly related to
ordinary phenomena.
b. little, if anything, had come from the study of UrOs during 21 years
that added to scientific knowledge.
c. further extensive study of UrO sightings was not justified in the
expectation that science would be advanced thereby.
d. no evidence had come to light that UFO sightings might represent
a defence hazura.
The Ministry of Defence would not disagree with any of these findings.
3. Reports of UFOs are not in themselves regarded by the Department as necessarily
classified naterial; reports are, hovever, in common with all correspondence rereived
le sipoves ase
from the general public, treated as confidential and pre closed to public scrutiny
until such time as they would become available in accordance with the Public Records
Act ie at the end of 30 years. Hewever, Ao reports earlier than 1962 are held in the
Department.
4. Finally, the Ministry of Defence does not dismiss the possibility that intelligent
life could exist in outer space.
However, the UFO reports that have reached the
Department to date contain no evidence to support this hypothesis.

YUMBES
THE REAL UFOS
139
would it be seen flying low over the coast near Haverfordwest,
because the debris didn't come down anywhere near Britain;
neither can a piece of even the smallest space debris hover - it
falls with the speed dictated by gravity.
I took an unprecedented step and ordered a number of radar
tapes to be impounded and sent to me. As these tapes are usually
wiped for reuse, it was important to work fast. There were a
few returns which fitted the times and locations when sightings
were made and after several hours of scouring the standard
VHS videos I could isolate and identify these. At first, the
results were disappointing. The blips faded in and out all
night, like ghosts in the morning light. RAF radar experts
explained these conventionally enough. Ground clutter, they
said, tall trees picked up now and again around one particular
radar head. But the frustration turned to fear: there were too
many visual sightings, and the reports were from witnesses too
trustworthy to ignore. Whatever it was that zigzagged Britain
on 30 and 31 March 1993, that probed our fields and raced our
cars, it was not picked up by radar. And consequently, with no
radar track to set the procedure in motion, we hadn't even got
our aircraft into the air. Was this the same triangle that had been
seen over Belgium three years earlier? And could it now evade
radar altogether?
Over the coming weeks I tried to find an explanation, but every
avenue led nowhere. Whatever it was had come and gone. It
was time to take the whole problem 'upstairs. Frankly, I didn't
hold out much hope that my bosses would listen. As I have
said, my hands-on approach and my firm views that we were
facing in UFOs a genuine phenomenon that needed serious and
urgent research had not met with popularity in Secretariat (Air
Staff). Subtlety was the key word, I felt. I drew up a carefully
constructed report of the 30-31 March sightings and sent it to my
head of division. I deliberately avoided the emotive word 'UFO',

===== PAGE 292 =====
{"alpha_words": 449, "chars": 2780, "method": "ocr", "page": 292, "variant": "plain-vision"}
OPEN SKIES. CLOSED MINDS
140
inspirer of prejudices, and opted instead for 'uncorrelated target'
and 'unknown craft'. Government bureaucracies are sometimes
accused of inventing jargon for the sake of it, but here I felt it
was necessary, it paid off. The report was passed up the chain
of command until it reached the assistant chief of the air staff
himself.
Simultaneously, I contacted the American embassy and asked
them whether an unusual prototype aircraft of American con-
struction was operating over Britain and might explain the
various sightings. There had been rumours for months in
the corridors of power that an aircraft called Aurora, which
would make the Stealth bomber look like a Sopwith Pup, was
in production. There had been consistent denials everywhere,
however. A high-tech, radar-evading craft capable of great speeds
and manoeuvrability, the sort of machine Clint Eastwood flies in
Firefox, belonged to fiction. Aurora, we were told, did not exist.
The Americans were as nonplussed as we and the Belgians were
by the sightings.
The assistant chief of the air staff noted my report - there was
little else he could do. By now I had tried all possible lines of
inquiry. There were no other avenues left.
So the official findings (mine) read:ỊiType of craft - unknown;
origin of craft - unknown; motive of occupants - unknown.'
And, although it appears nowhere in the official documentation,
I would have to add: 'Conclusion - unsatisfactory?
The 30-31 March sightings brought about a marked change in
my own attitude. I would play no further part in bland platitudes
about UFOs being 'of no defence significance'. I sensed that some
of my colleagues thought UFOs were only of defence significance
if they aimed laser beams at cities. But any craft, conventional or
otherwise, that can do what that triangle did is of extreme defence
significance in itself. Our radar couldn't trace it, our jets wouldn't
be able to catch it. We can all thank our God - or our lucky stars
- that whatever it was it
